behavior.\n</system-reminder>\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"57e0e27f-81e1-49c3-940f-5ebeb50f0c74","uuid":"b4d51ca9-62d7-42f8-af1b-c7c64ed839a8","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/app/data/handbook.pdf","content":"%PDF-1.3\r%����\n232 Inc., 1992 \n    65→1 \n    66→\n    67→\n    68→--- Page 4 ---\n    69→IN \n    70→I - TECHNOLOGY \n    71→~ \n    72→HOW DO YOU ACHIEVE HIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINTS? \n    73→The process of producing high quality solder joints is \n    74→similar to the process of learning how to ride a bicycle. \n    75→If you do not understand the fundamentals, it is virtually \n    76→impossible. However, once you understand and imple- \n    77→ment the basics, the results will follow. The goal of all \n    78→SMT process engineers is to repeatedly produce high \n    79→quality solder joints that have fine grain structure and \n    80→excellent wetting characteristics, as shown in the figure \n    81→below: \n    82→SURFACES MUST BE \n    83→CLEAN AND SOLDERABLE \n    84→II \n    85→MUST BE \n    86→1 \n    87→SMALL \n    88→CONTROL VARIABLES \n    89→In order to produce high quality solderjoints, all process \n    90→variables must be in control. The five general process \n    91→variables (l) that need to be in control include the mate- \n    92→rials, machines, manpower, methods, and the environ- \n    93→ment. If the process variables are not in control, there is \n    94→a very good chance that soldering defects will exist. \n    95→Let’s take a closer look at each. \n    96→MATERIALS \n    97→The materials must meet functional specifications so \n    98→that the product will perform to specification. Of equal \n    99→importance is the solderability of the assembly. The \n   100→solder paste/flux system must be compatible with the \n   101→base materials of the parts to be soldered. The solder \n   102→paste and parts should be as fresh as possible to enhance \n   103→solderability. \n   104→MACHINES \n   105→The machines used in the process must be able to perform \n   106→to their specifications. The machines should operate \n   107→within the range for which they are designed. Pushing a \n   108→machine beyond its operating limits will ultimately cause \n   109→a problem. The machines must be reliable and service- \n   110→able. \n   111→MANPOWER \n   112→The manpower must be properly trained. All machines in \n   113→the line require knowledgeable operators and mainte- \n   114→nance personnel. A machine is only as good as the person \n   115→operating it. \n   116→METHODS \n   117→The methods and procedures must be defined and strictly \n   118→adhered to. Inventory procedures should be implemented \n   119→to ensure that components and boards do not stay on the \n   120→shelf too long. Problems should be fully documented \n   121→including all data that pertains to it. \n   122→ENVIRONMENT \n   123→Finally, the processing environment must be controlled. \n   124→This includes both the environment inside and outside of \n   125→the machines. For example, if the humidity in the solder- \n   126→ing area is too high, this can cause the solder paste to \n   127→absorb moisture, whichmay cause problems duringreflow . \n   128→If the temperature in the room is not controlled, the \n   129→thermal profile in the reflow oven may be affected, which \n   130→again may cause a defect. \n   131→The purpose of this text is to address one aspect of high \n   132→quality SMT soldering, the reflow process. \n   133→Producing high quality solder joints \n   134→requires that five general process \n   135→variables need to be in control; the \n   136→materials, machines, manpower, \n   137→methods, and the environment. \n   138→2 \n   139→Section I - Technology \n   140→\n   141→\n   142→--- Page 5 ---\n   143→WHAT IS THE IMPORTANCE OF THE PROFILE? \n   144→I \n   145→I \n   146→I \n   147→I \n   148→I \n   149→I \n   150→1 ;  \n   151→I \n   152→:I \n   153→I \n   154→I \n   155→I \n   156→I \n   157→In order to fully understand Surface Mount Technology \n   158→(SMT) reflow, one needs to have an understanding of \n   159→what is being accomplished. One can take too simplistic \n   160→a viewpoint in that all you need to do is to heat solder \n   161→to a temperature greater than its melting point. One can \n   162→also take too complicated a viewpoint in believing that a \n   163→certain wavelength of infrared (IR) is required, or that a \n   164→certain percentage of convection is required. Perhaps the \n   165→most astute comment made on surface mount reflow was \n   166→by an individual whom had just completed an evaluation \n   167→of vapor phase, panel IR, and lamp IR equipment nearly \n   168→five years ago. After coming up with virtually identical \n   169→soldering defect results on all three types of soldering \n   170→equipment, the following comment was made: “If you \n   171→could hit the correct reflow profile using buckets of hot \n   172→water, you would accomplish high quality, low defect \n   173→solder joints.” Although the above comment was made \n   174→in jest (hot water is not quite hot enough to reflow solder), \n   175→there is considerable truth to what was said. \n   176→I \n   177→REFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT \n   178→High quality, low defect soldering requires identifying \n   179→the optimum temperature profile for reflowing the solder \n   180→paste, and repeating over and over. Every solder joint on \n   181→every board, needs to be heated similarily if the desired \n   182→soldering results are to be accomplished. It does not \n   183→matter how you apply the heat to the solder joint. What \n   184→does matter is that the heat is applied to the solder joint \n   185→in a controlled manner. The heating and cooling rise \n   186→rates must be compatible with the solder paste and \n   187→components. The amount of time that the assembly is \n   188→exposed to certain temperatures must be defined and \n   189→maintained. In other words, the solder reflow profile \n   190→must first be defined and then maintained. \n   191→280- \n   192→260- \n   193→240- \n   194→T 220- \n   195→High quality, low defect soldering \n   196→requires identifying the optimum \n   197→temperature profile for reflowing \n   198→the solder paste, and repeating it \n   199→over and over. \n   200→I WETTING ; \n   201→‘ \n   202→TIME \n   203→, \n   204→30-60 .’ \n   205→: \n   206→SEC \n   207→~ \n   208→TYPICAL PROFILE \n   209→A typical profile consists of a preheat, dryout, and reflow \n   210→section (zone) where different heating rates are used. In \n   211→the preheat section, the goal is to fully preheat the SMT \n   212→assembly to temperatures generally between 100°C to \n   213→150°C. The most critical parameter in the preheat section \n   214→is to minimize the rate of rise to less than 2”C/second. \n   215→The main concern is minimizing thermal shock on the \n   216→components of the assembly. Multilayer ceramic chip \n   217→capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if heated too \n   218→fast. Solder paste can spatter also, if heated too fast. \n   219→The second heating section, referred to as the dryout, \n   220→soak, or preflow zone, is used primarily to ensure that \n   221→the solder paste is fully dried before hitting reflow \n   222→temperatures. This zone also acts as a flux activation \n   223→zone for some solder pastes. Solder reflow is accom- \n   224→plished in the reflow zone, where the solder paste is \n   225→elevated to a temperature greater than the melting point \n   226→of the solder. For 63/37 Eutectic solder, the melting \n   227→temperature is 183°C. This temperature must be ex- \n   228→ceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure quality reflow. \n   229→The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting \n   230→point is referred to as the “wetting time”. The wetting \n   231→time is normally 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes. If the \n   232→wetting time is excessive, intermetallics may occur in \n   233→the joint, which result in brittle solder joints. In sum- \n   234→mary, define the profile, and hit it. \n   235→Section I - Technology \n   236→3 \n   237→\n   238→\n   239→--- Page 6 ---\n   240→HOW MANY HEATING ZONES ARE REQUIRED? \n   241→This question frequently arrives and can be a source of \n   242→confusion. One theory is that if ten zones are good, then \n   243→twenty zones are better. While in theory this may be true, \n   244→in practicality it may not be true. While it improves the \n   245→flexibility of oven, it also adds to the complexity and \n   246→makes oven set-up more difficult. \n   247→Let’s review this question from the standpoint of how \n   248→many zones are really needed. Reflowing solder paste \n   249→typically requires two or three different heating rates to \n   250→accomplish the process. In the figure below, there are \n   251→three “typical” profile bands for RMA, water soluble, \n   252→and no-clean solder pastes. These profiles are rather \n   253→generic, and one should consult with their solder paste \n   254→vendor to determine the desired profile. All of the pastes \n   255→in the figure could be successfully reflowed using \n   256→three zones. \n   257→reflow is that if you can control your top and bottom \n   258→heaters separately, then double sided reflow can be \n   259→accomplished (without reflowing side A twice). This \n   260→will not work with most SMT reflow processes, as most \n   261→products are too thermally thin. Independent topbottom \n   262→heating control does help in machines with dual convey- \n   263→ors that are located at different heights in the chamber. \n   264→Product warpage may also be minimized through proper \n   265→control. The bottom line is that most machines have this \n   266→feature, but most often the top and bottom heaters will be \n   267→set at the same value. \n   268→Edge heating zones become significant when edge con- \n   269→veyors are used. Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the \n   270→edge of the product. Two zones of edge heat (one front, \n   271→one back) will assist in compensating for edge con- \n   272→veyors. If mesh conveyors are used, these zones are \n   273→unnecessary unless the product width is quite wide. \n   274→A second issue is the question of independent top and \n   275→bottom heat control. A common conception in SMT \n   276→200 c \n   277→I \n   278→PREHEAT1 \n   279→I \n   280→PREHEAT2 \n   281→I REFLOW \n   282→I \n   283→I \n   284→I \n   285→I \n   286→I \n   287→I \n   288→I \n   289→I \n   290→I \n   291→I \n   292→I \n   293→COOLING I \n   294→TIME \n   295→4 \n   296→Section I - Technology \n   297→\n   298→\n   299→--- Page 7 ---\n   300→WHAT IS THE PROFILE PROCESS BAND? \n   301→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather a band or \n   302→process window.(*) The size of the band is defined by the \n   303→range of temperature deviation that can occur during \n   304→reflow and yield high quality results. This is indicated \n   305→by the crosshatched area shown in the figure. In the \n   306→example, the profile band size is indicated as 25°C. \n   307→The actual size of the band will vary depending upon \n   308→solder paste, component type, and circuit board material. \n   309→SOLDER SPATTER \n   310→AND SOLDER BALLS \n   311→CAUSED BY INSUF- \n   312→FICIENT DRYING. \n   313→BURNT BOARDS, \n   314→DEWET SOLDER fi \n   315→COLD SOLDER \n   316→JOINTS, DULL \n   317→SOLDER JOINTS \n   318→(TOO COLD) \n   319→REFLOW SOLDERING DEFECTS \n   320→If the product temperature profile is not maintained \n   321→within the band, a defect will occur. Chip capacitors \n   322→may crack if the rise rate in the preheat zone is exces- \n   323→sive. Solder spatter and solder balls may occur if drying \n   324→is excessive or insufficient. If drying is excessive, the \n   325→solder paste may skin over and not allow any of the \n   326→volatiles to escape. As the temperature of the solder joint \n   327→continues to heat, the vapor pressure increases until the \n   328→solder joint virtually explodes as the gases escape rap- \n   329→idly. The result is solder spatter and solder balls. If the \n   330→preheat is insufficient, solder spatter and balls may occur \n   331→in the reflow zone when higher heating rates occur. It is \n   332→critical that the paste is dried at low controlled heating \n   333→rates. In the reflow zone, insufficient heat will result in \n   334→non-reflowed or partially reflowed solder joints. The \n   335→joints will have a dull, rough surface finish in this case. \n   336→Excessive heating in the reflow zone may result in burnt \n   337→boards, damaged components, or possibly solder \n   338→dewetting. In a dewet solder joint, the solder is reflowed \n   339→but it is pulled away from the pad or the component lead. \n   340→DEFINE THE BAND \n   341→The profile band needs to be defined for the given SMT \n   342→assembly. To define this band, one should be aware of \n   343→not only the solder paste requirements, but also any \n   344→specific requirements of components or the circuit board \n   345→material. The process band width is defined as the total \n   346→deviation in temperature that can occur and yield reliable \n   347→results. \n   348→OVEN PERFORMANCE \n   349→From a process standpoint, the reflow oven must be able \n   350→to produce consistent results within the reflow profile \n   351→band. The user should be aware of all variables that \n   352→influence the band. These variables may be defined as \n   353→product related and oven related. By far the most critical \n   354→product related non-uniformity is that created by large \n   355→mass differences on the product. Since it is easier to heat \n   356→an area that has no components as compared with an area \n   357→with large components, temperature differentials will \n   358→exist on the product. Machine related non-uniformities \n   359→such as repeatability under product load and edge-to- \n   360→center and front-to-back product heating uniformities \n   361→must also be considered. To achieve high quality, low \n   362→defect soldering results, the sum of all the non-uniformi- \n   363→ties must fall within the defined reflow profile band. \n   364→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather \n   365→a range of temperatures all solder joints \n   366→must be exposed. \n   367→The total temperature deviation from \n   368→component thermal mismatch, oven load- \n   369→ing, and oven uniformity must be within \n   370→the band. \n   371→Section I - Technology \n   372→5 \n   373→\n   374→\n   375→--- Page 8 ---\n   376→WHAT ARE THE CAUSES OF NON-UNIFORM HEATING? \n   377→In order to fully comprehend the capability to heat within \n   378→the defined process band, one needs to better understand \n   379→the causes of heating non-uniformities. The three main \n   380→causes of non-uniform heating in the SMT reflow pro- \n   381→cess include mass differential on the product, conveyor \n   382→and heater edge effects, and product loading. \n   383→MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   384→The amount of temperature rise of a product as it travels \n   385→through a heating zone depends upon a number of \n   386→variables. The temperature rise of an object subjected to \n   387→heat is determined by the following equation: \n   388→AT = Q x A X  t / (M x Cp) \n   389→WHERE: \n   390→AT = Product Temperature Rise (“C) \n   391→Q = Heat Absorbed (w/cmz) \n   392→A = Exposed Area (cm2) \n   393→t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds) \n   394→M = Mass of Object (kg) \n   395→Cp = Specific Heat (w-sec/kg-”C) \n   396→The mass (M) times the specific heat (Cp) is often referred \n   397→to as thermal mass. Greater thermal mass requires more \n   398→heat to achieve a given temperature rise. In the figure \n   399→shown below, the area populated with the PLCC’s has \n   400→considerably more thermal mass per unit area than the \n   401→area with the discrete components. This area will be more \n   402→difficult to heat. The non-uniformity caused by thermal \n   403→mass differential may be measured by thermocoupling a \n   404→lead on the largest and smallest device, and running a \n   405→profile. \n   406→HIGH MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   407→J \n   408→m m m m  \n   409→m m m m  \n   410→m m m m  \n   411→m m m m  \n   412→m m m m  \n   413→m m m m  \n   414→OVEN UNIFORMITY \n   415→A second main cause of non-uniform product heating is \n   416→the oven heating pattern. This can be caused by edge \n   417→effects on the heaters (less heat at the end) or by heat \n   418→sinking of the conveyor system. The edge effect may be \n   419→caused by insufficient airflow around the edges of the \n   420→machine, or by non-uniformities that are inherent in the \n   421→heaters. The heat sinking of the conveyor is really amass \n   422→effect, similar to the one described above. The oven \n   423→uniformity may be measured by thermocoupling a bare \n   424→board over a matrix of points, and running a profile. The \n   425→matrix should include points on the board edges (front, \n   426→back, left, and right), and the center of the board. \n   427→OVEN REPEATABILITY \n   428→Repeatability refers to the oven’s capability to repeat a \n   429→given profile. Repeatability is affected by machine \n   430→loading. The figure below shows a typical product \n   431→loading condition. The “Loading Factor” is defined in \n   432→the figure. \n   433→PRODUCT FLOW INTO OVEN b \n   434→L \n   435→LOADING FACTOR = L+s \n   436→The higher the value of the loading factor, the more \n   437→difficult it is for any oven to give repeatable results. \n   438→Ovens will generally specify a maximum loading factor \n   439→(ranging from 0.5 to 0.9). The loading factor becomes an \n   440→important consideration when sizing an oven. The non- \n   441→uniformity caused by repeatability can be measured by \n   442→running a profile on a test board, then load the oven \n   443→down. Periodically, run the test board within the oven \n   444→load and compare with the unloaded profile. \n   445→The three main causes of non-uniform heating in \n   446→the SMT reflow process include mass differential \n   447→on the product, conveyor and heater edge effects, \n   448→and product loading. \n   449→6 \n   450→Section I - Technology \n   451→\n   452→\n   453→--- Page 9 ---\n   454→WHAT ARE THE MODES OF HEATING? \n   455→There are three different heating modes involved wit \n   456→most SMT reflow processes; convection, conductior \n   457→and infrared radiation (IR). All three of these heatin \n   458→modes occur naturally in our daily lives. Perhaps th \n   459→easiest way to understand each of these heating mode \n   460→is through example. \n   461→CONVECTION \n   462→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid (such a \n   463→air, nitrogen, or water) passes over a solid (such as a \n   464→SMT assembly). A cool breeze on a hot sunny da \n   465→provides convective cooling. Hot air from a hair drye \n   466→provides convective heating. Convection heating c \n   467→cooling requires contact of the flow with the solid par \n   468→Only the layer of the flow that is in contact with the pa \n   469→is actually transferring heat. \n   470→2ONVECTION I - \n   471→HOT \n   472→. \n   473→AIR \n   474→\\ \n   475→FLOW - \n   476→COLD OBJECT IS \n   477→- \n   478→HEATED BY CONVECTION \n   479→FROM THE HOT AIR FLOW \n   480→CONDUCTION \n   481→Conduction heat transfer occurs when two solid masse \n   482→of different temperature are in contact with each other \n   483→A good example is when a pan is placed on an electril \n   484→burner. Most of the heat is transferred to the pan by thl \n   485→contact between the pan and the burner. Conduction alsc \n   486→occurs within the same mass if a temperature differen \n   487→tial exists within the mass. \n   488→Referring to a system as 100% convection or \n   489→100% IR is incorrect. A system may be dominated \n   490→by convection or IR, but the other heat transfer is \n   491→always present in signijicant quantities. \n   492→CONDUCTION \n   493→HEAT FLOWS FROM HOT TO COLD TO HELP \n   494→EQUALIZE TEMPERATURES \n   495→RADIATION \n   496→Infrared Radiation (IR) occurs when two bodies of dif- \n   497→ferent temperature are in sight of each other. The best \n   498→example of IR is the heating of the earth by the sun. IR \n   499→is a non-contact heat transfer. \n   500→RADIATION I \n   501→In SMT reflow applications (with the exception of vapor \n   502→phase), convection, conduction, and IR all play a role in \n   503→the heating process. Referring to a system as 100% \n   504→convection or 100% IR is incorrect. A system may be \n   505→dominated by convection or IR, but the other heat trans- \n   506→fer is always present. IR dominant systems usually range \n   507→in the convection/IR ratio of 60/40 to 40/60. Convection \n   508→dominant systems have ratios in the range of 70/30 to \n   509→80/20. Conduction heat transfer occurs more within the \n   510→product during the heating process. Heat is transferred \n   511→through the product to help minimize hot or cold spots. \n   512→Section I - Technology \n   513→7 \n   514→\n   515→\n   516→--- Page 10 ---\n   517→HOW DOES INFRARED HEATING WORK? \n   518→95.38 \n   519→Infrared (IR) heat transfer occurs when two objects at \n   520→different temperatures are in sight of each other. The \n   521→heat is transferred by electromagnetic waves of 0.78- \n   522→1000 micron wavelengths. All objects emit some level \n   523→of infrared. The quantity of infrared energy emitted and \n   524→the wavelength of the emission are both dependent upon \n   525→the absolute temperature of the object. As the source \n   526→temperature increases, the heat transfer output increases \n   527→exponentially to the fourth power. Increasing the source \n   528→temperature results in shorter wavelengths. Decreasing \n   529→the source temperature results in longer wavelengths. In \n   530→SMT reflow the amount of heat transfer is important, so \n   531→as not to exceed the 2OC/sec rate. When operating at \n   532→these low heating rates all types of radiant emitters \n   533→(lamps, panels, tubes) operate in the medium to long \n   534→wavelength ranges. \n   535→1\n   536→1\n   537→1\n   538→1\n   539→1\n   540→1\n   541→l\n   542→I\n   543→ \n   544→INFRARED HEATING EQUATION \n   545→In order to understand what parameters are important in \n   546→infrared heating, one can consider the general equation \n   547→for heat transfer between the heat source and the object \n   548→being heated. Again, the purpose is not to memorize the \n   549→equation, but rather to point out the significance of what \n   550→is involved. The general equation for heat transfer is as \n   551→follows: \n   552→Q = V x Es x At x K x (Ts4 - Tt4) \n   553→WHERE: \n   554→Q = Infrared heat transfer (w/cm2) \n   555→V = Geometric view factor (0-1) \n   556→Es = Emissivity factor of the source (0-1) \n   557→At = Absorptivity factor of the target (0-1) \n   558→K = Stefan-Boltzmann Constant \n   559→Ts = Source temperature (OK) \n   560→Tt = Target temperature (OK) \n   561→(5.67 x \n   562→w/cm2/”K4) \n   563→Absorption characteristics of solder \n   564→paste and circuit board material makes \n   565→IR an efficient means of heating. \n   566→The geometric view factor “V” is the fraction of energy \n   567→that leaves the source that hits the target. In SMTreflow, \n   568→the oven chamber designs yield very high view factors \n   569→in the range of 0.90 to 0.95. An important aspect of the \n   570→view factor comes in product design. If two very large \n   571→components are in close proximity to each other, the \n   572→view factor to a solder joint between them is decreased, \n   573→which makes it more difficult. Refer to figure. \n   574→f \n   575→IRSOURCE \n   576→THIS AREA IS SHADED BY / \n   577→ADJACENT COMPONENTS \n   578→RESULTING IN A LOWER VIEW \n   579→FACTOR \n   580→The source emissivity and the target absorptivity factors \n   581→are in the range on 0.90 to 0.95 for most SMT applica- \n   582→tions. Solder paste is an excellent absorber of infrared \n   583→energy. Shiny gold components may be difficult to heat \n   584→as they tend to be reflective. Most often, however, the \n   585→board material, solder paste, and the components all \n   586→absorb quite well. The figure below shows the spectral \n   587→absorptivity for an RMA solder paste.(3) \n   588→I \n   589→loooo t \n   590→! 97.39 I4 I \n   591→96’70 \n   592→96.04 I! \n   593→Control of infrared is generally done by controlling the \n   594→source temperature. Providing that the overall source \n   595→emission can be regulated, IR can provide high levels of \n   596→repeatability. \n   597→8 \n   598→Section I - Technology \n   599→\n   600→\n   601→--- Page 11 ---\n   602→HOW DOES CONVECTION HEATING WORK? \n   603→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid at a given \n   604→temperature contacts a solid mass at a different tempera- \n   605→ture. If the fluid is hotter than the mass, the mass will be \n   606→heated. If the fluid is cooler than the mass, the mass will \n   607→be cooled. Perhaps the easiest way to understand convec- \n   608→tion is to look at the convection equation and note what \n   609→is important and what is not important. \n   610→CONVECTION EQUATION \n   611→The following is the general equation for convection \n   612→heating or cooling: \n   613→Q = H x A T  \n   614→WHERE: \n   615→Q = Heat absorbed per unit area (w/cm2) \n   616→H = Convective film coefficient \n   617→AT = Temperature difference between \n   618→(W/cm2-”C) \n   619→fluid and object (“C) \n   620→The amount of heat transfer “Q’ can be modified by \n   621→either changing the convective coefficient “H’ or the \n   622→temperature difference “AT”. Increasing the source \n   623→temperature will increase the temperature difference, \n   624→and thus the amount of heat transfer “Q’ will increase \n   625→proportionally. \n   626→The convective coefficient “H’ can have a significant \n   627→impact on the heat transfer “Q”. The value of “H” is \n   628→related to a number of variables, including velocity of \n   629→the flow and angle of attack of the flow. \n   630→Increasing the flow velocity will increase the value of \n   631→“H”. For example, when one is outside in the wind, a \n   632→low velocity cool breeze on a warm day will feel quite \n   633→nice. However, a strong wind on that same day may feel \n   634→~ \n   635→~~ \n   636→~~ \n   637→Perpendicular flow convection breaks \n   638→up surface thermal boundary layers, \n   639→which enhances heat transfer. \n   640→very cold. That is because the higher velocity wind has \n   641→a higher convective coefficient, and thus removes heat \n   642→more effectively from your skin. In SMT reflow appli- \n   643→cations, increasing the velocity of the flow also improves \n   644→the amount of convection heat transfer. There are prac- \n   645→tical limits to this, as too high of velocity may cause \n   646→components to shift around. The flow direction also has \n   647→a significant impact on “Q’. Parallel flow convection \n   648→is shown below: \n   649→PARALLEL FLOW WIBOUNDARY LAYER \n   650→I FLOW DIRECTION 4 - \n   651→BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   652→CAUSES STAGNANT FLOW NEAR \n   653→BOARD, WHICH REDUCES \n   654→HEAT TRANSFER \n   655→Note that in the parallel flow diagram, there is an area \n   656→referred to as the “boundary layer”. In the boundary \n   657→layer, there is no flow motion, which causes convective \n   658→heat transfer to be very low. It is beneficial to break up \n   659→the boundary layer to allow the flow to transfer heat \n   660→freely to the object. The diagram shown below shows \n   661→perpendicular flow convection: \n   662→PERPENDICULAR FLOW \n   663→FLOW DIRECTION \n   664→t\n   665→t\n   666→t\n   667→ \n   668→NO BOUNDARY \n   669→LAYER \n   670→NO BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   671→ENHANCES CONVECTIVE HEAT \n   672→TRANSFER \n   673→In the diagram showing perpendicular flow, note that \n   674→there is no boundary layer. This is the reason that \n   675→perpendicular flow yields better convective heating or \n   676→cooling results. \n   677→Section I - Technology \n   678→9 \n   679→\n   680→\n   681→--- Page 12 ---\n   682→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF IR? ... CONVECTION? \n   683→In SMT reflow applications, there is no such thing as \n   684→pure convection or pure IR. The only way to achieve a \n   685→100% convection system would be to have gas flowing \n   686→over the product with no chamber walls. Of course this \n   687→in not practical. To have a 100% IR system, the chamber \n   688→would need to be operated in a vacuum. Again, this is not \n   689→the case. However, some systems are more dominated by \n   690→convection, while others utilize IR as the primary heat \n   691→source. For the sake of simplicity this text will refer to a \n   692→convection dominated machine as a convection ma- \n   693→chine, and an IR dominate machine as an IR machine. \n   694→CONVECTION \n   695→Both convection and IR have their unique advantages \n   696→and limitations. In convective systems, typically the \n   697→temperature of the flow is close to the desired tempera- \n   698→ture of the product in a given zone. This has the \n   699→advantage in that the product temperature will never \n   700→exceed the gas flow temperature. This is useful when \n   701→heating a product that has a large difference in the mass \n   702→of the components and distribution. In this case, the \n   703→larger components will continue to heat, while the smaller \n   704→components will not overheat. Convection heat transfer \n   705→is not thermally efficient. Air (or nitrogen) has a very low \n   706→heat capacity, which means that high volumes of flow \n   707→may be required to transfer the heat. One way to improve \n   708→efficiency and reduce the amount of process gas required \n   709→CONVECTION \n   710→Less sensitive to shading \n   711→Heats more uniformly \n   712→AT (Source-target) is small \n   713→Low thermal mass of air requires \n   714→high flow rates to transfer heat \n   715→Inefficient (20 to 30%) \n   716→Will heat leads first, increasing \n   717→chance of solder wicking. \n   718→is through the use of recirculation. In a recirculation \n   719→loop, the process gas from a zone is evacuated, then \n   720→reheated and re-input into the heating chamber. The \n   721→limitation to recirculating is that high nitrogen purity \n   722→levels are not typically attainable. There is also a concern \n   723→that if the gas is recirculated excessively, it may affect \n   724→soldering quality, as the atmosphere surrounding the \n   725→product becomes contaminated with process by-prod- \n   726→ucts. Convection increases the chance of solder wicking, \n   727→as typically the lead is heated faster than the circuit board. \n   728→IR \n   729→IR systems have the distinct advantage of high heating \n   730→efficiencies and low process gas flows required to reflow \n   731→solder. IR systems are normally very repeatable, and \n   732→have the capability to transfer high quantities of heat as \n   733→needed. This becomes quite useful when reflowing very \n   734→dense SMT assemblies or very heavy boards. The \n   735→limitation with IR is that a high temperature differential \n   736→between the source and the product is required to transfer \n   737→the heat. Products with high component mass differen- \n   738→tial do not heat uniformly. They must be run quite slowly \n   739→to accomplish reflow. IR systems are successful at \n   740→reflowing products with component sizes up to and \n   741→including 84 pin PLCC’s. Large connectors and pin grid \n   742→arrays are quite difficult to reflow on an IR dominated \n   743→system. \n   744→IR \n   745→Control source temperature only \n   746→High efficiency (60-70%) \n   747→Easy to control rate of rise \n   748→More sensitive to shading \n   749→Less uniform high mass differences \n   750→AT (Source-target) is large \n   751→10 \n   752→Section I - Technology \n   753→\n   754→\n   755→--- Page 13 ---\n   756→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF A NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE? \n   757→~ \n   758→Ease of cleaning flux \n   759→residues (RMA) \n   760→Reflow soldering in a nitrogen atmosphere is becoming \n   761→a significant process consideration. The issue is really \n   762→not the ability to reflow in nitrogen, but rather the ability \n   763→to reflow in the absence of oxygen. Heating solder in \n   764→the presence of oxygen will create oxides, which are \n   765→generally non-solderable surfaces. In past days of reflow \n   766→soldering, the solder paste consisted of aggressive enough \n   767→fluxes to remove oxides during the reflow process. The \n   768→future trend is moving away from strong fluxes in the \n   769→paste. The future trends include two major process \n   770→changes that are driving the need for reflow in nitrogen \n   771→atmospheres; fine pitch soldering and no-clean fluxes. \n   772→500 ppm \n   773→FINE PITCH REFLOW SOLDERING \n   774→Fine pitch soldering presents many challenges. In order \n   775→to obtain satisfactory paste print definition, the particle \n   776→sizes of tin and lead are becoming smaller. As the \n   777→particles become smaller, the ratio of surface area to \n   778→volume of the particles increases. This can be shown by: \n   779→RATIO = (n x D*) /(1/6 x n x D3) \n   780→RATIO = 6 / D \n   781→WHERE: \n   782→D = Particle Diameter \n   783→FOR THE SAME VOLUME (OR MASS) OF SOLDER, THERE IS \n   784→GREATER SURFACE AREA WITH SMALLER PARTICLES THAN \n   785→WITH LARGER PARTICLES. \n   786→Thus, as particles sizes decrease, the ratio increases. \n   787→With more available surface area in the solder paste, \n   788→there is a greater tendency towards oxidation. Keep in \n   789→mind that oxidation is a function of the metals involved, \n   790→the temperatures of the metal, the exposed surface area, \n   791→and the amount of oxygen present. The only variable \n   792→that one can control is the amount of oxygen present. \n   793→This can only be done through proper oven design. \n   794→NO-CLEAN SOLDERING \n   795→The second technology change that is driving the need \n   796→towards nitrogen atmospheres is the greater usage of no- \n   797→clean pastes. The rapid trend away from solvent cleaning \n   798→is creating this and other needs. No-clean soldering \n   799→requires a tight control of the process. All of the compo- \n   800→nents and boards must be highly solderable. The reflow \n   801→process must not add any significant oxidation to the \n   802→process. The fluxes used are very weak, and do not have \n   803→aggressive oxide cleaning action. In addition, there is no \n   804→cleaning that would remove any solder balls from the \n   805→product. These solder balls eventually can create short \n   806→circuits on the product if they are not removed. Reflowing \n   807→in a high purity nitrogen environment reduces the possi- \n   808→bility of creating solder balls. \n   809→A good question to ask is “How pure a nitrogen environ- \n   810→ment is required?” A recent study (4) by Colin Lea of the \n   811→National Physics Laboratory in the United Kingdom was \n   812→conducted to determine the affect of nitrogen purity on \n   813→the process. The conclusions drawn in the study are \n   814→listed below: \n   815→DESCRIPTION \n   816→I \n   817→PURITY LEVEL I \n   818→Significant reduction \n   819→in board discoloration \n   820→10,000 ppm \n   821→Reduction in solder \n   822→200 ppm \n   823→balling (RMA) \n   824→Reflow of low-residue \n   825→solder paste \n   826→< 100 ppm \n   827→The paper presents a number of excellent graphs that \n   828→show the affect of nitrogen purity on parameters includ- \n   829→ing flux spread, wetting force, wetting time, solder balls, \n   830→cleaning, residue, board discoloration, and micro-struc- \n   831→ture. It is recommended reading if your process may \n   832→require the use of nitrogen. You must determine the \n   833→nitrogen requirements for your process. The benefits of \n   834→using nitrogen definitely exist, however you must deter- \n   835→mine if the additional cost of nitrogen is worthwhile for \n   836→your process. \n   837→Section I - Technology \n   838→11 \n   839→\n   840→\n   841→--- Page 14 ---\n   842→1 SECTION II - EQUIPMENT SELECTION I \n   843→WHAT ARE THE STEPS IN THE SELECTION PROCESS? \n   844→When selecting reflow equipment, it is important to take \n   845→into consideration all aspects that may affect the process \n   846→and production. Try to avoid becoming too focussed on \n   847→one issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may not \n   848→lead you to the optimum decision. The recommended \n   849→steps in the selection process are described in the follow- \n   850→ing paragraphs. \n   851→DEFINE PROCESS BAND WIDTH \n   852→The first step is to define the acceptable process profile \n   853→band width for your SMT assemblies. Try to be as \n   854→accurate as possible when defining the band. If the band \n   855→is too restrictive, some aspect of production will be \n   856→negatively affected. If the band is too loose, a defect will \n   857→show up in the process. The band width should be \n   858→defined as a range of temperatures (\"C) that the entire \n   859→profile must fall within. Refer to page 4 for a more \n   860→detailed description. \n   861→DEFINE HEATING NON-UNIFORMITIES \n   862→The next step is to define causes of heating non-unifor- \n   863→mities that may occur in the process. These non-unifor- \n   864→mities are caused by mass differential, oven uniformity, \n   865→and oven repeatability under production loading condi- \n   866→tions. A set of tests must be conducted with the desired \n   867→product on the oven in question. The sum of the non- \n   868→uniformities (\"C) must be less than the profile band width \n   869→defined in the first step. Refer to page 5 for a more \n   870→detailed description. \n   871→SIZE THE OVEN PROPERLY \n   872→The oven must be sized to handle the production rate of \n   873→the pick-and-place equipment. The calculation is shown \n   874→below: \n   875→LINE SPEED - BOARDS PER MINUTE X LENGTH PER BOARD \n   876→(MINIMUM) - \n   877→LOAD FACTOR \n   878→Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \n   879→speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the \n   880→onload area of the reflow oven. \n   881→Try to avoid becoming too focussed on one \n   882→issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may \n   883→not lead you to the optimum decision. \n   884→In order for the oven to be properly sized, the process \n   885→speed defined below must be greater than the minimum \n   886→calculated line speed. This will ensure that bottlenecks \n   887→will not occur at the reflow oven. The process speed \n   888→should be determined through testing or by the following \n   889→equation: \n   890→OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH \n   891→PROCESS - \n   892→SPEED - \n   893→PROCESS DWELL TIME \n   894→DEFINE PROCESS GAS \n   895→If a nitrogen atmosphere is required for solderability or \n   896→no-clean processing (see page lo), one needs to consider \n   897→the purity required and the gas consumption. Be careful \n   898→how the purity is measured, such that it is not measured \n   899→at the gas inlet. \n   900→REVIEW CONVEYOR DESIGN \n   901→The conveying mechanism is perhaps the most over- \n   902→looked aspect of equipment selection. One needs to \n   903→define whether a mesh conveyor or an edge type is \n   904→required. Typically edge conveyors are used for in-line \n   905→processing or if double-sided assembly is required. The \n   906→most important issue with edge conveyors is the ability \n   907→for the conveyor to remain straight to avoid dropping \n   908→product. \n   909→RELIABILITY/SERVICEABILITY \n   910→Once a machine is placed into service, it is critical that it \n   911→remains operational. Evaluate the product design from a \n   912→structural as well as a functional standpoint. Is the oven \n   913→designed to withstand the rigors of production? Review \n   914→the product manuals, drawings, and spare parts lists. \n   915→Find out where the nearest servicing center is located. Is \n   916→training available? These are the questions that must be \n   917→asked during the selection process. \n   918→INTERFACING REQUIREMENTS \n   919→Conveyor and control interfacing requirements may also \n   920→be an important selection issue. Make sure that the edge \n   921→conveyor fixed rail justification is the same as the pick- \n   922→and-place equipment. Ask questions about networking \n   923→machines to a host and product flow control schemes if \n   924→needed. \n   925→12 \n   926→Section I1 - Equipment Selection \n   927→\n   928→\n   929→--- Page 15 ---\n   930→HOW CAN YOU EVALUATE COSTS? \n   931→DESCRIPTION \n   932→The selection steps listed on the previous page should \n   933→all be considered when comparing equipment. When \n   934→multiple ovens pass the selection criteria listed, then a \n   935→proper cost evaluation may be used to compare ovens. \n   936→This is especially important when comparing ovens that \n   937→utilize different heating technologies, as first cost and \n   938→operating costs can be significantly different. This \n   939→section describes various costs that may be included in \n   940→the analysis. \n   941→HOT GAS \n   942→IR \n   943→EQUIPMENT COST \n   944→Often, the initial cost of the equipment is the only cost \n   945→considered when comparing equipment. It is certainly \n   946→important, although it is only a part of the total cost \n   947→picture. To make a fair comparison, all costs should be \n   948→analyzed with a common basis, such as annual cost. As \n   949→an example let’s look at amachine that costs $50,000 and \n   950→has an expected life of 5 years, with an interest rate of \n   951→10%. (3 For an interest rate of 10% with a period of \n   955→5 years, the CRF value is equal to 0.2638. SECTION III - RESEARCH, INC. EQUIPMENT TECHNOLOGY~ \n  1069→CONVECTION OVEN THERMAL CONTROL \n  1070→Convection oven thermal control requires tight control \n  1071→of two variables; temperature and flow. In order to \n  1072→maintain constant heat flux to the product, it is critical \n  1073→that both these variables are controlled. Research, Inc.’s \n  1074→MICRI-FLO XG forced convection reflow system will \n  1075→be used as an example. The temperature and flow control \n  1076→are defined specific to this system. \n  1077→CONVECTION OVEN DESCRIPTION \n  1078→Temperature control of the system must consider both \n  1079→the gas and the surrounding wall temperature. Remem- \n  1080→ber, even in a convection dominant heating system, the \n  1081→walls radiate IR energy worth about 20%-30% of the \n  1082→total heat flux of the system. The figure below shows \n  1083→the heater cavity flow diagram for the MICRI-FLO XG. \n  1084→The reflow and dryout zones each receive input gas \n  1085→(air or nitrogen) through a series of flow meters. This \n  1086→controlled flow enters a diffusion plenum behind the \n  1087→heating plenum to distribute the flow. The flow then \n  1088→enters a second plenum, where the gas is preheated by a \n  1089→series of heaters. The heaters consist of a wound nickel- \n  1090→chromium wire encased in a stainless steel jacket. The \n  1091→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM \n  1092→AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \n  1093→II \n  1094→4 \n  1095→Y \n  1096→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \n  1097→heaters are bent in a “U” shape such that easy removal \n  1098→can occur from one side of the machine. The gas then \n  1099→enters the process chamber through an array of holes. \n  1100→The product is heated from a combination of the hot gas \n  1101→and the hot chamber surroundings. A control thermo- \n  1102→couple is mounted in each zone to maintain cavity \n  1103→repeatability. A PID (Proportional - Integral - Deriva- \n  1104→tive) control loop is used to maintain the cavity at \n  1105→constant conditions. \n  1106→The flow is removed from the reflow area by two exhaust \n  1107→ducts located on each side of the reflow zone. This flow \n  1108→is pulled from the zones using a high temperature, low \n  1109→flow recirculating blower. The flow is pushed by the \n  1110→blower through the preheater plenum arrangement. The \n  1111→temperature control is accomplished in the same way as \n  1112→the dryout and reflow zone. \n  1113→The flow that heats the product (in all zones) is forced \n  1114→through the walls in an array of holes to break up the \n  1115→boundary layers on the product. The perpendicular flow \n  1116→arrangement shown on page 8 is used to optimize heat \n  1117→transfer. \n  1118→Convection oven thermal control requires tight \n  1119→control of two variables; temperature andflow. \n  1120→In order to maintain constant heat flux to the \n  1121→product, it is critical that both these variables \n  1122→are controlled. \n  1123→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1124→15 \n  1125→\n  1126→\n  1127→--- Page 18 ---\n  1128→CONVECTION OVEN WITH \n  1129→Convection ovens are a greater challenge to inert for \n  1130→several reasons. First, the amount of flow that would be \n  1131→required to transfer 80% of the energy by convection is \n  1132→extremely high, and cost prohibitive. In order to mini- \n  1133→mize this, recirculation ducts are added, which result in \n  1134→the degradation of the purity of the source flow entering \n  1135→a given zone. Higher velocities and turbulent flow are \n  1136→also required, which can result in a greater opportunity \n  1137→for oxygen to enter the system. Third, the pressure in the \n  1138→heater cavity is positive in some areas, and negative in \n  1139→others. Balancing this flow becomes adifficult task. The \n  1140→job of inerting a convection oven is not impossible. \n  1141→However, it should be noted that purity levels and flow \n  1142→rates are less optimum than with an IR based machine. \n  1143→CHAMBER DESCRIPTION \n  1144→Many of the same principles apply for inerting when \n  1145→comparing a convection oven to an IR oven. The MZCRZ- \n  1146→FLO XG convection oven shown below utilizes a very \n  1147→similar scheme to the 4470XN IR oven. The gas is \n  1148→brought into each zone very uniformly using the propri- \n  1149→VITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n  1150→etary gas plenum distribution system. The pressure in the \n  1151→entry and exit purge sections are maintained at sufficient \n  1152→levels to minimize oxygen infiltration. Flow meter \n  1153→controlled venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow \n  1154→in the system and to remove process volatiles. \n  1155→In order to maintain the best purity levels in the zones \n  1156→where the product temperature is greater than 150°C, \n  1157→pure nitrogen is delivered to the reflow and dryout \n  1158→zone. This is a key point, as this ensures that the product \n  1159→experiences a nearly pure environment at its most criti- \n  1160→cal stages (dryout and reflow). The recirculated gas \n  1161→from these zones is reheated and used in the preheat zone. \n  1162→Here, purity is not as critical, so the use of recirculated \n  1163→gas is not detrimental to the process. \n  1164→Purity levels in the dryout and reflow zone can be \n  1165→maintained at 35 PPM over incoming purity levels at \n  1166→flow rates of 1800 cubic feet per hour (5 1 cubic meters \n  1167→per hour). \n  1168→GAS FLOW \n  1169→INPUT FROM \n  1170→N2 OR AIR \n  1171→SOURCE \n  1172→GAS FLOW \n  1173→INPUT FROM \n  1174→N2 OR AIR \n  1175→SOURCE \n  1176→16 \n  1177→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1178→\n  1179→\n  1180→--- Page 19 ---\n  1181→IR/CONVECTION OVEN CLOSED LOOP CONTROL \n  1182→One of the key oven performance issues is the ability to \n  1183→repeat a product temperature profile. Repeatability is \n  1184→influenced most by product loading. Heavy or non- \n  1185→variable loading can result in the oven incorrectly com- \n  1186→pensating, resulting in a loss of repeatability. High levels \n  1187→of repeatability can be achieved only if all the compo- \n  1188→nents of heat transfer are maintained constant. \n  1189→LAMP DEFINITION \n  1190→The closed loop heat flux control method used in a \n  1191→Research, Inc. lamp based oven will be used as an \n  1192→example of one type of control. The primary heat source \n  1193→in this system is the tungsten filament (T3) lamp. The T3 \n  1194→lamp consists of a tungsten filament encased in a sealed \n  1195→quartz envelope. The envelope is filled with an inert gas \n  1196→to keep the filament from oxidizing and failing. The \n  1197→lamps are rated for 5000 hours when operated at 100% \n  1198→capacity. In SMT applications, the lamp power never \n  1199→exceeds 20% capacity, resulting in lamp life in excess of \n  1200→20,000 hours. At typical operating setpoints of 5-15%, \n  1201→the heat transfer to the product is controlled such that \n  1202→heating rates are under 2”C/second, and the IR emission \n  1203→is in the medium to long wavelength range. \n  1204→The greatest advantage that T3 lamps have is their rapid \n  1205→response capability. Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage \n  1206→change occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature equi- \n  1207→librium. This rapid response is ideal for closed loop \n  1208→control, as energy can be replaced in the heating chamber \n  1209→instantly after its removal. \n  1210→CONTROL DEFINITION \n  1211→In order for the product profile to be repeatable, the total \n  1212→heat flux to the product must be held constant. The total \n  1213→heat flux to the product is the sum of IR from the lamp and \n  1214→wall plus the convective component. Refer to the figure. \n  1215→Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage change \n  1216→occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature \n  1217→equilibrium. This rapid response is ideal for \n  1218→closed loop control, as energy can be replaced \n  1219→in the heating chamber instantly after its \n  1220→removal. \n  1221→RAPID-RESPONSE (EXPOSED-TIP) \n  1222→THERMOCOUPLE \n  1223→PRODUCT ”‘7 \\ R E F L F  \n  1224→JON. \n  1225→$ RE-EMITTED \n  1226→IR \n  1227→VECTION \n  1228→1 \n  1229→FLOW -- \n  1230→\\P \n  1231→PRODUCT \n  1232→Accurate contol is not achieved by maintaining only one \n  1233→of the components constant, but rather the sum of all the \n  1234→thermal energies involved. The lamp IR component is \n  1235→regulated by the SCRpower controlled system. The wall \n  1236→and convective components are collectively monitored \n  1237→by the bulk chamber temperature measured with a ther- \n  1238→mocouple probe. There are two reference setpoints; \n  1239→percent voltage for the lamp, and the bulk chamber \n  1240→temperature. The bulk chamber setpoint drives the \n  1241→equation. If the chamber is at the correct temperature, \n  1242→then the lamp setpoints will be at their setpoints. If the \n  1243→chamber is too hot, then the lamps will decrease in \n  1244→intensity. If the chamber is too cool, the lamps will \n  1245→operate at a level greater than their setpoint. The lamp \n  1246→power is modified only enough to compensate for the \n  1247→bulk chamber heat transfer deviation. In doing so, the \n  1248→total heat flux to the product is maintained. The correct \n  1249→compensation will occur providing the bulk chamber \n  1250→temperature is within k 25°C. Product temperature \n  1251→repeatability o f f  2.5”C will occur if the bulk chamber \n  1252→is within this defined band. \n  1253→The ability to operate within this wide band has two \n  1254→significant advantages. The first is that the oven may \n  1255→be loaded down to levels up to 80% load. The second is \n  1256→that profile changeover times are reduced significantly. \n  1257→Loading may begin when all oven temperatures are \n  1258→within f 25°C of setpoint. This requires far less time to \n  1259→achieve the “ready” state than if the “ready” process \n  1260→window was f 3”C, which is often the case inreflow oven \n  1261→operation. Warm-up times of less than 15 minutes occur. \n  1262→Profile changeover times are 3 to 5 minutes for profile \n  1263→increases of under 40”C, and under 10 minutes for profile \n  1264→decreases of under 40°C. Even in the worst case of going \n  1265→from a reflow profile to an adhesive cure profile, the \n  1266→changeover time is under 15 minutes. \n  1267→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1268→17 \n  1269→\n  1270→\n  1271→--- Page 20 ---\n  1272→IR/CONVECTION OVEN WITH NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n  1273→PREHEAT TOP (IR1) \n  1274→Infrared ovens do not require high quantities of flow to \n  1275→accomplish reflow. The predominant heat transfer oc- \n  1276→curs with IR, which does not require any flow to transfer \n  1277→the heat. The flow in the oven is used to provide some of \n  1278→the benefits of convection, which includes improving \n  1279→populated board uniformity and volatile removal. The \n  1280→amount of flow in the oven is more related to the quantity \n  1281→required to accomplish a high purity nitrogen environ- \n  1282→ment, which is considerably less than in the case of a \n  1283→convection oven. \n  1284→REFLOW \n  1285→TOP (IR5) \n  1286→DRY TOP (IR3) \n  1287→PREFLOW TOP (IR4) \n  1288→NITROGEN OVEN DESCRIPTION \n  1289→The 4470XN from Research, Inc. is an example of an IR \n  1290→dominant oven that provides a high purity nitrogen \n  1291→environment at low flow levels. \n  1292→The system uses a \n  1293→proprietary gas flow distribution method through the \n  1294→chamber walls to uniformly blanket the chamber with the \n  1295→process nitrogen. A slight positive pressure inside the \n  1296→chamber helps minimize external room effects and helps \n  1297→keep oxygen out of the chamber. Entry and exit purge \n  1298→sections are present to provide a nitrogen curtain at the \n  1299→chamber entrance and exit, which also assists in keeping \n  1300→oxygen out of the chamber. Flow meter controlled \n  1301→venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow in the \n  1302→system and to remove process volatiles. \n  1303→PREHEAT BOTTOM (IR2) \n  1304→The gas system is non-recirculatory, in that the gas is \n  1305→used once and then expended. This has the advantage \n  1306→that process contaminants are not recirculated which \n  1307→helps maintain high nitrogen purity levels. The heat \n  1308→chamber is accessible by unfastening six clamps and \n  1309→elevating the top heater vertically through the use of a \n  1310→crank mechanism. Purity levels of 20 PPM (over in- \n  1311→coming purity level) are attainable in all heating zones. \n  1312→Flow rates are in the 1200 to 1600 cubic feet per hour \n  1313→(34 to 45 cubic meters per hour) range. Machines in the \n  1314→field are experiencing total purity levels of 10-15 PPM \n  1315→(including incoming) at flow rates of 1300 cubic feet per \n  1316→hour (37 cubic meters per hour). These purity levels are \n  1317→measured with the machine fully loaded with product. \n  1318→REFLOW \n  1319→BOTTOM (1R6) \n  1320→DRY BOTTOM (IR3) \n  1321→PREFLOW BOTTOM (IR4) \n  1322→~~~ \n  1323→~ \n  1324→The amount of flow in the oven is more \n  1325→related to the quantity required to accom- \n  1326→plish a high purity nitrogen environment, \n  1327→which is considerably less than in the case \n  1328→of a convection oven. \n  1329→NOTE: VENTURI EXHAUSTS ARE ACTIVATED BY COMPRESSED AIR, \n  1330→ALL OTHERS MAY BE NITROGEN OR COMPRESSED AIR. \n  1331→18 \n  1332→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1333→\n  1334→\n  1335→--- Page 21 ---\n  1336→EDGE CONVEYOR \n  1337→Proper design of the edge conveying system is critical, \n  1338→as typically only 0.17 inches (4 mm) is in contact with \n  1339→the bottom side edges of the product. If the conveyor \n  1340→has lateral warpage of greater than this amount, the \n  1341→product will fall into the oven. Thus, edge conveyor \n  1342→straightness is a key issue in SMT processing. \n  1343→As the conveyor rails heat during the machine warm-up \n  1344→period, they will grow and tend to warp. The patented \n  1345→system from Research, Inc. shown below tensions the \n  1346→rails, which pulls the rail assembly straight. The lever \n  1347→(outlined in dark) is attached to the rail. The bottom side \n  1348→of the lever is attached to an adjustable spring mechanism \n  1349→that runs from end to end in the machine. Approximately \n  1350→1500 lbs (681 kg) of tensioning force is applied to each \n  1351→rail to maintain straightness. The machine frame is made \n  1352→of welded structural tubing in order to maintain its \n  1353→structural integrity. \n  1354→The use of the tensioning system allows the mass of the \n  1355→rails to be maintained at a reasonably low value, which \n  1356→minimizes the thermal affect of the rail on the product. \n  1357→As the conveyo’r rails heat during the ma- \n  1358→chine warm-up period, they will grow and \n  1359→tend to warp. The patented system from \n  1360→Research, Inc. shown below tensions the \n  1361→rails, which pulls the rail assembly straight. \n  1362→- \n  1363→TENSION STRAIGHTENS RAIL \n  1364→SPRING MAINTAINS TENSION \n  1365→REFERENCES \n  1366→1. “The Transformation of American Industry”, Productivity-Quality Systems, Inc.
